      1173 +                                                             
      1174 +def acc_summary(results: list[dict[str, Any]]) -> dict[str, A
           +ny]:                                                         
      1175 +    """ACC-MCP use and the decision model's online miss rate 
           +over all acc runs."""                                        
      1176 +    acc_runs = [r for r in results if r["mode"] == "acc"]    
      1177 +    ledgers = [r["acc"] for r in acc_runs if r.get("acc")]   
      1178 +    with_silent = [a for a in ledgers if a.get("silent_escala
           +tions") is not None]                                         
      1179 +    slices = sum(a["requests"] for a in with_silent)         
      1180 +    misses = sum(a["escalations"] + a["silent_escalations"] f
           +or a in with_silent)                                         
      1181 +    return {                                                 
      1182 +        "runs": len(acc_runs),                               
      1183 +        "used": sum(1 for a in ledgers if a["requests"]),    
      1184 +        "without_hint": sum(1 for r in acc_runs if r.get("hin
           +t") is False),                                               
      1185 +        "slices": sum(a["requests"] for a in ledgers),       
      1186 +        "served_tiers": {t: sum(a["served_tiers"][t] for a in
           + ledgers) for t in ("1", "2", "3")},                         
      1187 +        "model_tiers": {t: sum(a["model_tiers"][t] for a in l
           +edgers) for t in ("1", "2", "3")},                           
      1188 +        "explicit_escalations": sum(a["escalations"] for a in
           + ledgers),                                                   
      1189 +        "silent_escalations": sum(a["silent_escalations"] for
           + a in with_silent) if with_silent else None,                 
      1190 +        "online_miss_rate": round(misses / slices, 4) if slic
           +es else None,                                                
      1191 +    }

      1306 +def render_report(runs: list[tuple[str, list[dict[str, Any]],
           + dict[str, Any] | None]], kinds: dict[str, str]) -> str:     
      1307 +    """eval/results.md: one section per run id, every run rep
           +orted (PROTOCOL.md section 9)."""                            
      1308 +    lines = [

      1310 +        "Plain Pi vs Pi + ACC-MCP, operated by hand in intera
           +ctive Pi (method and hypotheses H1-H3: "

           +ot part of the results.", "",                                
      1312 +        "- **H1 (navigation cost):** navigation calls before 
           +the first edit of the target function, and "

           +sed`/... output plus ACC-MCP slices; characters / 3.8 "      
      1314 +        "for both modes).",                                  
      1315 +        "- **H2 (success):** resolved tasks.",               
      1316 +        "- **H3 (peak context):** largest prompt of one model
           + call, as llama-server reported it to Pi.", "",              
      1317 +    ]                                                        
      1318 +    for run_id, results, setup in runs:                      
      1319 +        lines += render_run(results, run_id, setup, kinds)   
      1320 +    return "\n".join(lines)

          +ead_tokens, acc_tokens=0, acc=None, navigation=True):         
      460 +    """A result.json as collect writes it, with only the field
          +s the report reads."""

          +ens": 30000, "model_calls": 10, "minutes": 2.5,

          +d_tokens}

      475 +        }                                                     
      476 +    return result

  Called gitnexus (ctrl+o to expand)
⏺ I've built the tooling for Phase 26, but none of the benchmark steps can be
  ticked yet. The counted runs (26.2, 26.4) are yours to do in interactive Pi.
  26.1 and 26.3 are done as tooling but still need you to run freeze and report.
  I haven't run the new tests.

  What I built
  - 26.1, pi_bench.py freeze: records a run id's setup in
    runs/<run-id>/setup.json: ACC-MCP commit, model file, quantization (read
    from the model file name), llama-server build, Pi version, context window
    and session budget, plus checksums of tasks.json and PROTOCOL.md.
    - It refuses if the model isn't running, Pi is missing, a clean test suite
      passes 0 tests, or src/ or the eval files have uncommitted changes.
    - After freezing, prepare refuses a run whose context window or tasks.json
      no longer matches.
    - This also settles the Q4 vs Q6 question for the results, since the
      quantization is read from the actual model file.
  - 26.3, report rewritten: for each run id, results.md now shows:
    - the frozen setup (or a "not frozen" warning);
    - summaries per mode and per task kind, with the hypothesis columns:
      resolved (H2), navigation calls before the target edit and code-reading
      tokens (H1), and peak context (H3), plus wasted and large reads;
    - ACC-MCP use, tiers, explicit and silent escalations, and the online miss
      rate;
    - a paired plain / acc table per task;
    - every run with your notes.

    --run-id can be given several times, so every run gets reported. A missing
    or unmeasured value shows as "–", never 0.
  - Tests: 15 new tests in tests/test_pi_bench.py, counting each parametrized
    case. They cover reading the quantization from a file name, the freeze
    checks, refusing a run that no longer matches, the report tables with values
    worked out by hand, and results collected before Phase 24.
  - PROTOCOL.md sections 6 and 11 now describe freeze and the multi-run report.

  What's left, in order
  1. Finish Step 25.5 first. setup and validate haven't been re-run:
     eval/work/baseline/click.json still shows 0 passed, and the click metadata
     stub isn't in the test venv. freeze will refuse until that's fixed.
  ! python3 eval/pi_bench.py setup
  ! python3 eval/pi_bench.py validate
  ! python3 eval/pi_bench.py doctor
  ! .venv/bin/python -m pytest -q
  2. Commit this tooling, because freeze refuses a dirty tree. Suggested name:
     feat(eval): freeze run setup and report navigation metrics per mode and 
     task kind.
  3. Freeze, run, report: run python3 eval/pi_bench.py freeze, then for each of
     the 10 tasks do plain then acc (prepare → start.sh → collect --notes), then
     report.
  4. Optional (26.4): repeat the set as bench2, and do the --no-hint acc runs
     under their own run id (e.g. nohint1). Freeze each new run id first.
  5. Run pytest and commit the results as feat(eval): Pi benchmark results for 
     plain vs ACC-MCP.

      15  
      16  ## 1. Executive Summary & Problem Formulation
      17  
      18 -Small local models such as **Qwen3.5-4B** run with a small cont
         -ext window on a laptop (this project uses 30,208 tokens, the pe
         -r-slot window `llama-server` reports on the reference machine).
         - A coding agent that reads whole source files into that window 
         -fills it quickly:                                              
      19 -1. **Context bloat:** a full window leaves less room for the ta
         -sk, and an overfull request fails outright.                    
      20 -2. **Token waste:** many questions (checking a signature, a typ
         -e hint, or who calls a function) need only the interface, not t
         -he function bodies.                                            
      18 +Small local models such as **Qwen3.5-4B** run with a small cont
         +ext window on a laptop (this project uses 30,208 tokens, the pe
         +r-slot window `llama-server` reports on the reference machine).
         + Inside a coding agent, the model does not usually read whole f
         +iles into that window. It **navigates**: it searches with `grep
         +`/`find`, then reads the file in line windows (`read` with `off
         +set`/`limit`), and some of those windows land in the wrong plac
         +e. Every search and every window is another tool call whose out
         +put stays in the conversation.                                 
      19  
      22 -**Adaptive Context Compressor (ACC-MCP)** is a Model Context Pr
         -otocol (MCP) server that sits between the agent and the code. I
         -nstead of returning raw files, a **Decision Model** picks how m
         -uch of a symbol to show (signatures, call graph, or full body) 
         -and serves that slice of the file. Rules written in code enforc
         -e a token ceiling, a per-session token budget, and which files 
         -may be read at all.                                            
      20 +This is what the pilot session showed (plain Pi, a bug in more-
         +itertools' `difference`, inside a 5,644-line file of about 45K 
         +tokens; the session is not part of the results). The agent neve
         +r read the whole file. It found the function with `find | xargs
         + grep` and `grep -n`, then read it in 6 windows of 10-100 lines
         +, 2 of which missed the function. Code reading was 10 of its 65
         + tool calls; 48 were its own Python experiments.               
      21  
      22 +**Adaptive Context Compressor (ACC-MCP)** is a Model Context Pr
         +otocol (MCP) server that gives the agent **symbol-level navigat
         +ion** instead. `find_symbol` says where a class, function or me
         +thod is defined, and `get_adaptive_context` returns exactly tha
         +t symbol at the depth the task needs: signatures (Tier 1), sign
         +atures plus callers and callees (Tier 2), or the verbatim body 
         +with its real line numbers (Tier 3). A **Decision Model** picks
         + the tier from the task intent. Rules written in code enforce a
         + token ceiling, a per-session token budget, and which files may
         + be read at all.                                               
      23 +                                                               
      24 +Whether this makes a small model **cheaper at finding and readi
         +ng code without making it worse at fixing bugs** is what Block 
         +9 measures, against the agent's own `grep` + windowed `read` st
         +rategy (hypotheses H1-H3 in [`eval/PROTOCOL.md`](eval/PROTOCOL.
         +md)). Whole-file reading is not assumed; the benchmark counts i
         +t (large reads).                                               
      25 +

      105  - **Exposed Tools:**
      106    1. `get_adaptive_context(file_path, task_intent, symbol_name
           =None, min_tier=None)`: checks the path, runs the decision mod
           el, and returns the slice. `min_tier` (1–3) lets the agent ask
            for more context when a slice was not enough; it can raise th
           e model's tier but never lower it. Each request is recorded in
            the ledger.
      107    2. `find_symbol(name)`: finds where a class, function, or me
           thod is defined anywhere in the workspace and returns file pat
           h (relative to the workspace root), qualified name, kind, and 
           line range as JSON (at most 50 matches).
      108 -  3. `get_token_ledger()`: returns all-time totals, `escalatio
          -n_rate`, the current session budget, and the recent request hi
          -story as JSON.                                                
      108 +  3. `get_token_ledger()`: returns served tokens per tier and 
          +per tool, `escalation_rate`, the current session budget, the w
          +hole-file comparison as an upper bound, and the recent request
          + history (with the line ranges each slice showed) as JSON.    
      109    4. `reset_session()`: starts a fresh session token budget; a
           ll-time totals and history are kept.
      110  - **Evidence:** In-process tool tests in [`tests/test_server.p
           y`](tests/test_server.py) (discovery, each tool, errors, `min_
           tier`, budget, ledger persistence across restarts). No test ye
           t starts the server as a subprocess or drives it from a real a
           gent; that is planned in Phase 14.

      149    - $x_2$ (complexity) and $x_4$ (annotations) get weights nea
           r zero. Setting either to 0 changes at most 3 train and 2 hold
           out tiers, because many $x_1 = 1$ tasks sit so close to $\thet
           a_{\text{high}}$ that a tiny shift moves them.
      150    - $x_3$ (in-degree) gets a **negative** weight. For $x_1 = 1
           $ it decides between Tier 2 (the symbol has callers) and Tier 
           3 (it has none), and changes 10 train and 4 holdout tiers. Thi
           s comes from the data, not from a design idea; with this datas
           et, more callers does not mean more context is needed.
      151    - Most errors come from the keyword classifier, not the weig
           hts. Intents with no keyword fall back to $x_1 = 1$. Caller qu
           estions phrased with inspection words ("Find every function th
           at calls…") get $x_1 = 0$. Body edits phrased with Tier 2 verb
           s ("Add retry logic inside…") get $x_1 = 1$.
      152 -- **Limits of this measurement:** the labels and intents were 
          -written by the project author, not collected from users. Train
          - and holdout tasks can share a code snippet, so $x_2$–$x_4$ va
          -lues are not fully unseen. Only 2 intents are real (from the s
          -erver ledger); real Pi intents will be added once the Phase 14
          - runs exist.                                                  
      153 -- The real-world signal is `escalation_rate` in `get_token_led
          -ger`: the share of requests where the agent had to raise `min_
          -tier` above the model's choice because the slice it got was no
          -t enough.                                                     
      152 +- **Limits of this measurement:** the labels and intents were 
          +written by the project author, not collected from users. Train
          + and holdout tasks can share a code snippet, so $x_2$–$x_4$ va
          +lues are not fully unseen. Only 2 intents are real (from the s
          +erver ledger); the `task_intent` strings the model sends in th
          +e Block 9 acc runs will be added once those runs exist.       
      153 +- **Online signal (Block 9):** in real use a miss shows up in 
          +two ways. An **explicit escalation** is the agent calling agai
          +n with a `min_tier` above the model's tier (`escalation_rate` 
          +in `get_token_ledger`). A **silent escalation** is the agent g
          +etting a Tier 1 or Tier 2 slice and then reading some of the s
          +ame lines itself with `read`/`sed` within the next 5 tool call
          +s. The **online miss rate** is (explicit + silent) / slices se
          +rved, reported in `eval/results.md` next to the offline holdou
          +t numbers above. **Not measured yet:** it needs the counted Pi
          + runs.                                                        
      154 +- **Which tiers the benchmark exercises:** bug fixes need the 
          +body, so the decision model mostly picks Tier 3 for them. In 6
          + of the 10 benchmark tasks the report points at the function t
          +o fix. The other 4 are call-graph tasks, where the report name
          +s a function whose helper has the bug, so that Tier 1 and Tier
          + 2 (signatures, callers, callees) are used at all.            
      155  
      156  ### Block 5: Memory or Context Budget Management (`src/acc_mcp
           /budget.py`, `src/acc_mcp/index.py`)
      157  - **Session Budget:** All slices in a session share a token bu
           dget (`ACC_MCP_SESSION_BUDGET`, default 6,000 tokens, about 20
           % of the 30,208-token window; the benchmark sets exactly 20%, 
           6,042). Each slice is cut to $\min(C_{\max}, \text{tokens left
           })$. If the chosen tier does not fit, the server serves one ti
           er lower and says so in a `# [ACC-MCP] Served Tier n instead o
           f Tier m` note; a tier forced with `min_tier` that does not fi
           t is refused with a `# [ACC-MCP] Not served` message. `reset_s
           ession` starts a new budget.

      155  
      156  ### Block 5: Memory or Context Budget Management (`src/acc_mcp
           /budget.py`, `src/acc_mcp/index.py`)
      157  - **Session Budget:** All slices in a session share a token bu
           dget (`ACC_MCP_SESSION_BUDGET`, default 6,000 tokens, about 20
           % of the 30,208-token window; the benchmark sets exactly 20%, 
           6,042). Each slice is cut to $\min(C_{\max}, \text{tokens left
           })$. If the chosen tier does not fit, the server serves one ti
           er lower and says so in a `# [ACC-MCP] Served Tier n instead o
           f Tier m` note; a tier forced with `min_tier` that does not fi
           t is refused with a `# [ACC-MCP] Not served` message. `reset_s
           ession` starts a new budget.
      158 -- **Ledger:** Every request is recorded with its served tier, 
          -model tier, `min_tier`, and token counts. Savings per request 
          -are measured against reading the whole file:                  
      159 -  $$\text{Savings (\%)} = \frac{\text{Tokens}_{\text{Full File
          -}} - \text{Tokens}_{\text{Served Tier}}}{\text{Tokens}_{\text{
          -Full File}}} \times 100\%$$                                   
      160 -  The ledger is saved once per request to the file named by `A
          -CC_MCP_LEDGER`, or to `context_budget.json` in the server's wo
          -rking directory. Totals cover all requests; the history keeps 
          -only the last 200 records.                                    
      158 +- **Ledger:** Every request is recorded with its served tier, 
          +model tier, `min_tier`, served tokens, and the line ranges it 
          +showed (file, symbol, first and last line; for a slice cut by 
          +the ceiling, only the lines really shown). The summary reports
          + what the server really knows: served tokens per tier and per 
          +tool (`get_adaptive_context`, `find_symbol`). The comparison w
          +ith the whole file is kept only as a labelled **upper bound** 
          +(`whole_file_tokens`, `upper_bound_savings_pct`):             
      159 +  $$\text{Upper-bound saving (\%)} = \frac{\text{Tokens}_{\tex
          +t{Whole File}} - \text{Tokens}_{\text{Served}}}{\text{Tokens}_
          +{\text{Whole File}}} \times 100\%$$                           
      160 +  It is an upper bound because it assumes the agent would othe
          +rwise read the whole file, which it did not do in the pilot se
          +ssion. The saving that Block 9 tests is against the agent's ow
          +n `grep` + windowed `read` strategy. Ledgers written before th
          +is change (`baseline_tokens`, `savings_percentage`) still load
          +. The ledger is saved once per request to the file named by `A
          +CC_MCP_LEDGER`, or to `context_budget.json` in the server's wo
          +rking directory. Totals cover all requests; the history keeps 
          +only the last 200 records.                                    
      161  - **Workspace Symbol Index (`index.py`):** Walks every servabl
           e `.py` file under the workspace root (skipping `.venv`, `venv
           `, `.git`, `__pycache__`, `node_modules`) and stores each file
           's symbol map (names, line ranges, complexities, callers, call
           ees, raised exceptions) in the ledger. Each file's SHA-256 is 
           compared with the cached `file_hash`: unchanged files are reus
           ed, changed files are re-parsed, deleted files are dropped.
      162  - **Cold Restart Test:** After a restart, the index is rebuilt
            from the ledger and unchanged files are not parsed again (the
            tests count parser calls). The file served by `get_adaptive_c
           ontext` is still parsed on each request, because slices are cu
           t from its AST.
      163  - **Evidence:** [`tests/test_budget.py`](tests/test_budget.py)
           , [`tests/test_index.py`](tests/test_index.py), and the budget
            tests in [`tests/test_server.py`](tests/test_server.py).

      165  ### Block 9: A Measured Comparison (Manual Evaluation with Pi)
      166 -- **Question:** does ACC-MCP change whether a small local mode
          -l fixes a bug in real code, and how much context it uses, when
          - the model works inside a real coding agent (Pi)? The full met
          -hod is in [`eval/PROTOCOL.md`](eval/PROTOCOL.md).             
      166 +- **Question:** does ACC-MCP make it cheaper for a small local
          + model to find and read the code it needs, without making it w
          +orse at fixing the bug, when the model works inside a real cod
          +ing agent (Pi)? The full method is in [`eval/PROTOCOL.md`](eva
          +l/PROTOCOL.md).                                               
      167 +- **Hypotheses:**                                             
      168 +  - **H1 (navigation cost):** fewer navigation calls before th
          +e first edit of the target function, and fewer code-reading to
          +kens, than the agent's own `grep` + windowed `read` strategy. 
      169 +  - **H2 (success):** at least as many tasks resolved.        
      170 +  - **H3 (peak context):** a smaller largest prompt per model 
          +call. This may not change: in the pilot, most tool calls were 
          +the agent's own Python experiments, which ACC-MCP does not tou
          +ch.                                                           
      171  - **Why our own benchmark:** SWE-bench's grader needs a Docker
            image of several GB per task; Exercism-style exercises are si
           ngle small files where ACC-MCP has nothing to save; HumanEval/
           MBPP have no repository at all. The benchmark instead injects 
           bugs into real libraries (the SWE-smith approach) and grades t
           hem with the libraries' own tests, without Docker.

      181  - **Conditions:** every task is done twice with the same model
           , prompt, tools, compaction settings, operator rules and time 
           limit. Only the acc run adds the ACC-MCP server (tools declare
           d directly) and one sentence telling the model to prefer it fo
           r reading code.
      182  - **Grading:** after restoring the original tests, a task is r
           esolved when the target tests pass and the full suite has no f
           ailures beyond those of the clean repo.
      183  - **Measured per run:** resolved or not; navigation calls befo
           re the first edit of the target function, code-reading tokens,
            wasted reads and large reads (the agent's own `grep` + window
           ed `read` strategy is the baseline, not reading whole files); 
           peak context of one model call, total prompt tokens, model cal
           ls, tools used, compactions, wall time; and (with ACC-MCP) tie
           rs served, explicit and silent escalations, and budget step-do
           wns.
      184 -- **Helper script:** [`eval/pi_bench.py`](eval/pi_bench.py) (`
          -setup`, `validate`, `doctor`, `prepare`, `collect`, `list`, `r
          -eport`). It never drives Pi itself.                           
      185 -- **Status: not run yet.** Results will go to `eval/results.md
          -`; until then this README makes no claim about token savings o
          -r task success.                                               
      184 +- **Helper script:** [`eval/pi_bench.py`](eval/pi_bench.py) (`

  ⎿  Added 2 lines, removed 4 lines
       7  
       8  ## 1. Executive Summary & Motivation
       9  
      10 -Small local models such as **Qwen3.5-4B** run with a small cont
         -ext window on a laptop (this project uses 30,208 tokens, the pe
         -r-slot window `llama-server` reports on the reference machine).
         - When coding agents handle multi-file codebases, they often rea
         -d entire files into that window. This creates two problems:    
      11 -1. **Context Bloat:** a full window leaves less room for the ta
         -sk, and an overfull request fails outright.                    
      12 -2. **Token Waste:** many questions (verifying a function signat
         -ure, finding call sites, checking return types) need only the i
         -nterface, not the function bodies.                             
      10 +Small local models such as **Qwen3.5-4B** run with a small cont
         +ext window on a laptop (this project uses 30,208 tokens, the pe
         +r-slot window `llama-server` reports on the reference machine).
         + Inside a coding agent, the model finds code by **navigating**:
         + it searches with `grep`/`find` and reads files in line windows
         +, some of which miss the code it needs. Each search and each wi
         +ndow is a tool call whose output stays in the conversation. In 
         +the pilot session (excluded from the results) the agent never r
         +ead the ~45K-token file whole. It found the function with `grep
         +` and read it in 6 windows of 10-100 lines, 2 of which missed t
         +he function.                                                   
      11  
      14 -**Adaptive Context Compressor (ACC-MCP)** is a standalone Model
         - Context Protocol (MCP) server that acts as an intelligent toke
         -n-budget gatekeeper. Instead of returning raw source files, an 
         -embedded **Decision Model** predicts the minimum context fideli
         -ty needed for a specific task and serves a pruned AST (Abstract
         - Syntax Tree) slice. A deterministic code layer enforces strict
         - token ceilings and file security boundaries.                  
      12 +**Adaptive Context Compressor (ACC-MCP)** is a standalone Model
         + Context Protocol (MCP) server that gives the agent **symbol-le
         +vel navigation**. `find_symbol` locates a definition, and `get_
         +adaptive_context` serves exactly that symbol at the depth the t
         +ask needs. An embedded **Decision Model** picks that depth (sig
         +natures, call graph, or verbatim body), and a deterministic cod
         +e layer enforces token ceilings, a session budget and file secu
         +rity boundaries. Block 9 measures whether this makes finding an
         +d reading code cheaper than the agent's own `grep` + windowed `
         +read` strategy, without lowering the success rate.             
      13  
      14  ---
      15  

⏺ Update(Description.md)
  ⎿  Added 9 lines, removed 3 lines
       90  \text{Tier 3 (Full Body)}, & \text{if } P(\text{Full}) \ge \th
           eta_{\text{high}}
       91  \end{cases}$$
       92  
       93 -### 4.4 Token Savings Metric                                  
       94 -For each retrieval event, relative token reduction is computed
          - against the raw full-file read:                              
       93 +### 4.4 Measuring the Saving                                  
       94 +The ledger records what ACC-MCP served (tokens per tier and pe
          +r tool, and the line ranges of every slice). It also compares 
          +each slice with the whole file it came from, but only as an **
          +upper bound**:                                                
       95  
       96 -$$\text{Savings (\%)} = \frac{\text{Tokens}_{\text{Full File}}
          - - \text{Tokens}_{\text{Served Tier}}}{\text{Tokens}_{\text{Fu
          -ll File}}} \times 100\%$$                                     
       96 +$$\text{Upper-bound saving (\%)} = \frac{\text{Tokens}_{\text{
          +Whole File}} - \text{Tokens}_{\text{Served}}}{\text{Tokens}_{\
          +text{Whole File}}} \times 100\%$$                             
       97  
       98 +This assumes the agent would otherwise read the whole file, wh
          +ich it did not do in the pilot session. The saving that is tes
          +ted (H1 in `eval/PROTOCOL.md`) compares with what the agent re
          +ally does, measured from Pi's session file in both modes with 
          +the same counter (characters / 3.8):                          
       99 +                                                              
      100 +$$\text{Code-reading tokens} = \sum_{c \,\in\, \text{code\_rea
          +d}} \text{Tokens}_{\text{out}}(c) + \sum_{c \,\in\, \text{ACC-
          +MCP}} \text{Tokens}_{\text{out}}(c)$$                         
      101 +                                                              
      102 +together with the number of navigation calls (code reading plu
          +s `find_symbol` / `get_adaptive_context`) before the first edi
          +t of the target function.

⏺ Update(Description.md)

      125  * **Implementation:** A logistic model whose weights are fitte
           d by gradient descent on a train split; the thresholds are han
           d-picked.
      126  * **Evaluation:** 100 labeled task intents (29 Tier 1, 31 Tier
            2, 40 Tier 3), split 70/30 by tier. Results are reported sepa
           rately for train and for the 30-task holdout the fit never saw
           .
      127  * **Metrics:** 3x3 tier confusion matrix, false alarm rate (se
           rved a higher tier than needed), and miss rate (served a lower
            tier than needed). The holdout numbers are in `README.md`.
      128 +* **Online signal:** in the Block 9 acc runs, a miss shows up 
          +as an explicit escalation (the agent raises `min_tier`) or a s
          +ilent one (after a Tier 1/2 slice the agent reads the same lin
          +es itself within 5 tool calls). The online miss rate (explicit
          + + silent) / slices served will be reported next to the holdou
          +t numbers; it is not measured yet. Bug fixes mostly need Tier 
          +3, so 4 of the 10 benchmark tasks are call-graph tasks, to exe
          +rcise Tier 1 and Tier 2 at all.

⏺ Update(Description.md)
  ⎿  Added 2 lines, removed 2 lines
      130  ### Block 5: Memory or Context Budget Management
      131  * **Implementation:** A per-session token budget plus a ledger
            that persists across server restarts.
      132  * **Mechanisms:**
      133 -  * Caps the tokens served per session and steps down a tier w
          -hen the chosen one does not fit.                              
      134 -  * Persists the request history, totals, and the workspace sy
          -mbol index to a local `context_budget.json` ledger.           
      133 +  * Caps the tokens served per session (20% of the window in t
          +he benchmark) and steps down a tier when the chosen one does n
          +ot fit.                                                       
      134 +  * Persists the request history (with the line ranges each sl
          +ice showed), totals per tier and per tool, and the workspace s
          +ymbol index to a local `context_budget.json` ledger. The whole
          +-file comparison is kept only as an upper bound (section 4.4).
      135    * Restart test: after a restart the symbol index is rebuilt 
           from the ledger and unchanged files are not parsed again (the 
           file being served is still parsed for each request).

          +own `grep` + `read`), H2 (success not lower) and H3 (peak cont

          +s` tools count as code reading; a read of any other file count

✻ Worked for 11s · done 8:49 AM

❯ How many time approximately it will consume? I mean these tests               

⏺ About 4 to 8 hours for the 20 counted runs, so plan for a working day,
  possibly split across sessions. This is an estimate: the only timing data is
  the pilot.

  Basis
  - Pilot timing: the pilot run (plain mi_difference) took about 30 minutes,
    from 06:25 to 06:55 in its session file. Most of that went into 48 Python
    experiments, on a harder task than the current ones.
  - Time limit: the protocol stops every run at 30 minutes, so the worst case is
    20 × 30 min = 10 hours.
  - Easier tasks: the reports were made easier in Phase 25, so I'd expect most
    runs to take 10–25 minutes. Some acc runs may be faster if the model uses
    find_symbol.
  - Overhead per run: about 1–2 minutes for prepare, pasting the prompt, collect
    (which runs the target tests and the full suite, only seconds for these
    repos) and writing notes. That's roughly 30–40 minutes in total.

  ┌──────────────────────────┬──────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
  │           Part           │ Runs │               Estimate                │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Step 25.5 (setup,        │      │ about 5 minutes; validate runs both   │
  │ validate, doctor,        │  –   │ suites plus each task's tests twice   │
  │ pytest)                  │      │                                       │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ freeze                   │  –   │ seconds                               │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ bench1, the required     │  20  │ 4–8 h, worst case about 10.5 h        │
  │ runs                     │      │                                       │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ bench2, optional repeat  │  20  │ another 4–8 h                         │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ nohint1, optional, acc   │  10  │ another 2–4 h                         │
  │ only                     │      │                                       │
  └──────────────────────────┴──────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘

  Splitting it across days is fine as long as nothing changes between sessions:
  - Same llama-server settings: you can restart it, but keep the same model file
    and -c 30208. prepare refuses a different window; it does not check the
    model file, so keeping that the same is up to you.
  - No commits to tasks.json in between, since prepare also refuses a changed
    tasks.json.
  - Keep the pairs together: finish each task's plain and acc runs in the same
    session, so slow drift in the machine or in you affects both modes alike
    (PROTOCOL.md 5.1).

  python3 eval/pi_bench.py list shows where you stopped.

✻ Brewed for 12s · done 8:51 AM

❯ and what if we will have, another computation power?                          
  Like tesla T4? We can try to benchmark on Kaggle notebooks                    
  Or thats not realstic scenario?                                               

⏺ Yes, it's realistic, but only in one form. A T4 can serve the model; it can't
  easily host the manual protocol.

  The problem with running everything on Kaggle

  The protocol is manual: you watch interactive Pi in a terminal and send the
  prompt and Continue. yourself. A Kaggle notebook has no real interactive
  terminal for a full-screen app like Pi, and sessions are capped (about 12
  hours per session and a weekly GPU quota). Running Pi itself on Kaggle would
  mean automating the operator. That's a different protocol, and only possible
  if Pi has a non-interactive mode, which I haven't checked.

  The realistic setup: model on Kaggle, everything else local

  - On Kaggle: llama-server with the same GGUF file and -c 30208, exposed
    through a tunnel (cloudflared or ngrok).
  - Locally, unchanged: Pi, ACC-MCP, the repos and pi_bench.py, pointed at the
    tunnel with --endpoint https://<tunnel-url> on doctor, freeze and prepare.

  The code already supports this: --endpoint sets the server address, and freeze
  reads the window and model file from the remote /props. Check Kaggle's terms
  on exposing a server through a tunnel before relying on it.

  What you'd gain

  Only the model's share of the time gets faster. A T4 should be roughly 1.5–3×
  faster than a laptop for a 4B model, mostly on re-reading long prompts, which
  matters at ~25K tokens of context. That's a rough guess, not a measurement.
  Your own time (prompts, watching, notes, collect) stays the same, so expect
  maybe 3–5 hours instead of 4–8, not a dramatic cut. With two T4s you could run
  two llama-server instances, but you can only watch one Pi session at a time.

  What to keep fair

  - One machine per run id: speed doesn't change navigation calls, tokens or
    tiers, but it does change minutes. It also changes how far a run gets before
    the 30-minute cap, which can change "resolved". So all 20 runs of one run
    id must be on the same hardware: either all on the T4, or all local.
  - Record the hardware: use a separate run id (for example t4_bench1) and note
    the hardware. freeze doesn't record the GPU yet; I can add a --hardware 
    "Kaggle T4" field if you go this way.
  - Session cap: a session ending mid-run means redoing that task pair (prepare 
    --force), so start each Kaggle session with enough time left for several
    full pairs.

  My recommendation: if your laptop can host the model, do bench1 locally.
  That's the setup the protocol and docs describe ("a 16 GB laptop that also
  runs the model"), and a T4 saves maybe a couple of hours. The T4 becomes worth
  it for the optional repeats (bench2, nohint1), or if you later automate the
  operator: then many unattended repeats would give real statistics instead of
  one run per task.

      112 +def metrics_from_evaluations(evaluations: list[dict[str, Any]]
          +) -> EvaluationMetrics:                                       
      113 +    """Confusion matrix, accuracy, miss and false alarm rates 
          +from per-task predictions.                                    
      114 +                                                              
      115 +    Each evaluation needs `ground_truth_tier` and `predicted_t
          +ier`; it is kept as is in                                     
      116 +    `task_evaluations`. Shared by the Block 4 decision model a
          +nd the Block 7 intent models.                                 
      117 +    """                                                       
      118 +    tier_cm: dict[int, dict[int, int]] = {a: {p: 0 for p in TI
          +ERS} for a in TIERS}                                          
      119 +    for item in evaluations:                                  
      120 +        tier_cm[item["ground_truth_tier"]][item["predicted_tie
          +r"]] += 1                                                     
      121 +                                                              
      122 +    total = len(evaluations)                                  
      123 +    correct = sum(tier_cm[t][t] for t in TIERS)               
      124 +    misses = sum(tier_cm[a][p] for a in TIERS for p in TIERS i
          +f p < a)                                                      
      125 +    false_alarms = sum(tier_cm[a][p] for a in TIERS for p in T
          +IERS if p > a)                                                
      126 +    can_miss = sum(sum(tier_cm[a].values()) for a in TIERS if 
          +a > 1)                                                        
      127 +    can_over_serve = sum(sum(tier_cm[a].values()) for a in TIE
          +RS if a < 3)                                                  
      128 +                                                              
      129 +    per_tier: dict[int, dict[str, float]] = {}                
      130 +    for t in TIERS:                                           
      131 +        support = sum(tier_cm[t].values())                    
      132 +        predicted = sum(tier_cm[a][t] for a in TIERS)         
      133 +        per_tier[t] = {                                       
      134 +            "precision": _ratio(tier_cm[t][t], predicted),    
      135 +            "recall": _ratio(tier_cm[t][t], support),         
      136 +            "support": support,                               
      137 +        }                                                     
      138 +                                                              
      139 +    return EvaluationMetrics(                                 
      140 +        total_samples=total,                                  
      141 +        accuracy=_ratio(correct, total),                      
      142 +        misses=misses,                                        
      143 +        false_alarms=false_alarms,                            
      144 +        miss_rate=_ratio(misses, can_miss),                   
      145 +        false_alarm_rate=_ratio(false_alarms, can_over_serve),
      146 +        per_tier=per_tier,                                    
      147 +        tier_confusion_matrix=tier_cm,                        
      148 +        task_evaluations=evaluations,                         
      149 +    )

⏺ I've finished the code and tests for Phase 28 (steps 28.1–28.4), but I haven't
  run either. Step 28.5 needs the real holdout numbers in the README, and only
  running the script gives those. Please run:

  ! .venv/bin/python src/acc_mcp/intent_model.py
  ! .venv/bin/python -m pytest -q

  Paste the report (at least the Holdout (30 tasks) table and What Naive Bayes 
  Learned) and the pytest count. I'll then write the README Block 7 section with
  those numbers, whatever they are.

  What changed
  - src/acc_mcp/intent_model.py (new):
    - The model: Naive Bayes over the lowercased words of task_intent.
      Underscored names like calculate_hash are split into words; words never
      seen in training are ignored; Laplace smoothing; log-probabilities; no new
      dependencies. When two tiers score the same it picks the higher tier,
      matching the earlier choice that too little context is worse than too
      much.
    - Training and baselines: trained on the same 70/30 split and seed as
      Block 4. It's compared with two baselines on the same 30 holdout tasks:
      always the most common train tier (Tier 3), and the current keyword rule
      read directly as a tier.
    - Report: holdout first and train kept apart, a 3×3 confusion matrix for
      each model, the holdout errors of Naive Bayes next to the keyword rule's
      answer, and the words behind each tier for the defence. Besides the most
      likely words per tier, I added the most distinctive ones, because the most
      likely are shared words like "the".
  - evaluate_decision.py: the metric arithmetic moved out of
    DecisionEvaluator.evaluate into metrics_from_evaluations, so both blocks use
    the same code. GitNexus rated evaluate MEDIUM risk (callers: main,
    calibrate, the Block 4 tests). Its output is unchanged, and a new test
    checks that its per-task fields are still there.
  - tests/test_intent_model.py: 20 tests, counting each parametrized case:
    - fitting and predicting on a 6-intent hand-made set, with the smoothing
      values checked against hand calculations;
    - the model being fitted on exactly the train intents (its fit is
      intercepted to check);
    - all three models scored on the same holdout ids;
    - the metrics agreeing with the confusion matrix for every model and split;
    - two runs giving an identical report.
  - todo.md: steps 28.1–28.4 are ticked.

  Before you commit

  The Phase 27 doc edits are still uncommitted, so the GitNexus change check
  (MEDIUM risk, from the evaluate refactor) covers both phases. Suggested order
  once tests pass:
  1. docs: reframe ACC-MCP as symbol-level navigation (results pending)
  2. After I add the numbers: feat(model): learned intent classifier evaluated 
     against baselines

      235  ## Phase 30: Claimed Blocks 2, 3, 4, 5, 7, 8 (Documentation, R
           1-R4)

⏺ I've finished the code and tests for Phase 29 (steps 29.1–29.4), but I haven't
  run them. Step 29.5, like 28.5, needs real numbers for the README, so both
  wait on these runs:

  ! .venv/bin/python src/acc_mcp/intent_model.py
  ! .venv/bin/python src/acc_mcp/uncertainty.py
  ! .venv/bin/python -m pytest -q

  The second command takes about a minute because it refits the weights for 50
  splits. Paste the outputs (and the pytest count), and I'll write README Block
  7 and Block 8 with whatever the numbers are.

  What changed
  - src/acc_mcp/uncertainty.py (new, no new dependencies):
    - Intervals: Wilson 95% intervals and seeded bootstrap 95% intervals (2,000
      resamples of the 30 holdout tasks) for accuracy, miss rate and false alarm
      rate. They're computed for five models: fitted weights, hand-picked
      weights, Naive Bayes, the keyword rule, and the most-common-tier baseline.
    - Paired comparisons: on the same 30 tasks, an exact McNemar test and a
      paired bootstrap interval for the accuracy difference. One pair is fitted
      vs hand-picked weights (Block 4), the other Naive Bayes vs the keyword
      rule (Block 7).
    - Split sensitivity: 50 stratified splits, with mean, standard deviation,
      min and max holdout accuracy for the logistic model, Naive Bayes and the
      keyword rule, plus the seed-21 value for comparison. The features are
      computed once, since they don't depend on the split; only the weights are
      refitted.
    - The report ends with the "what the data cannot conclude" sentence: 30
      tasks, labels written by you, and train and holdout sharing code snippets.
  - tests/test_uncertainty.py: 24 tests, counting each parametrized case. All
    expected values were worked out by hand:
    - Wilson intervals (7/10 → 39.68–89.22%, 0/10, 10/10, 21/30), including that
      they're symmetric;
    - a fixed bootstrap seed gives the same interval, and the interval contains
      the estimate;
    - McNemar gives p = 1 for identical predictions and the hand-counted values
      (for example 1-vs-6 → 0.125);
    - the 50-seed sweep fits only on each seed's own train split (both fits are
      intercepted to check);
    - the seed-21 row equals the Block 4 and Block 7 single-split numbers.

  Things to know
  - Slower tests: the suite will be noticeably slower, roughly 10–20 seconds
    more, because several tests refit the weights at 5,000 epochs each. I kept
    those to as few seeds as possible.
  - GitNexus: no existing symbol was edited in this phase; it only adds two new
    files.

  Uncommitted: the Phase 27 docs, Phase 28 and Phase 29 are all still
  uncommitted. Once tests pass and I've added the numbers, the suggested commits
  are:
  1. docs: reframe ACC-MCP as symbol-level navigation (results pending)
  2. feat(model): learned intent classifier evaluated against baselines
  3. feat(eval): confidence intervals and paired comparison for decision models

  ## What the data cannot conclude                                              
  With 30 holdout tasks, labels written by the project author, and train and    
  holdout tasks that share code snippets, these numbers show how the models     
  behave on this dataset only; a difference whose paired interval includes 0    
  (or whose McNemar p is above 0.05) is not evidence that one model is better.  

⏺ Both reports are in. The headline: the learned Naive Bayes model does not beat
  the keyword rule. It reached 46.7% vs 70.0% on the seed-21 split and 59.4% vs
  69.2% averaged over 50 splits. A plain result to report, and the uncertainty
  run changes how the Block 4 number should be read as well.

      162  - **Cold Restart Test:** After a restart, the index is rebuilt
            from the ledger and unchanged files are not parsed again (the
            tests count parser calls). The file served by `get_adaptive_c
           ontext` is still parsed on each request, because slices are cu
           t from its AST.

      165 +### Block 7: A Learned Model Compared with Baselines (`src/acc
          +_mcp/intent_model.py`)                                        
      166 +- **Question:** Block 4 found that most decision errors come f
          +rom the keyword rule that turns `task_intent` into $x_1$, not 
          +from the weights. Can a model **learned from the intent's word
          +s** do better?                                                
      167 +- **Model:** multinomial Naive Bayes over the lowercased words
          + of `task_intent` (`snake_case` names split into words), Lapla
          +ce smoothing ($\alpha = 1$), log-probabilities, no new depende
          +ncies. Words never seen in training are ignored, and ties go t
          +o the higher tier. It is a separate artifact from the Block 4 
          +logistic model, evaluated offline only and **not used by the s
          +erver**.                                                      
      168 +- **Setup:** the same split as Block 4 (70 train / 30 holdout 
          +tasks, stratified, seed 21). Baselines on the same 30 tasks: a
          +lways the most common train tier (Tier 3), and the current key
          +word rule read directly as a tier (depth 0/1/2 → Tier 1/2/3). 
          +Run `python3 src/acc_mcp/intent_model.py`.                    
      169 +- **Holdout results (30 tasks):**                             
      170 +                                                              
      171 +  | Model | Accuracy | Miss rate | False alarm rate |         
      172 +  | :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: |                            
      173 +  | Naive Bayes (words of `task_intent`) | 46.7% | 47.6% (10) 
          +| 33.3% (6) |                                                 
      174 +  | Keyword rule (depth + 1) | **70.0%** | 38.1% (8) | **5.6%*
          +* (1) |                                                       
      175 +  | Most common train tier (Tier 3) | 40.0% | 0.0% (0) | 100.0
          +% (18) |                                                      
      176 +                                                              
      177 +  Naive Bayes holdout confusion matrix (rows = actual tier, co
          +lumns = predicted 1/2/3): Tier 1 `5 3 1`, Tier 2 `4 3 2`, Tier
          + 3 `3 3 6`. The keyword rule's: Tier 1 `8 1 0`, Tier 2 `1 8 0`
          +, Tier 3 `1 6 5`.                                             
      178 +- **It overfits:** 98.6% on its own 70 train tasks against 46.
          +7% on the holdout. With 70 short intents, most words occur in 
          +only one or two of them.                                      
      179 +- **What it learned:** the most likely words of every tier are
          + shared ones ("the", "does", "of", "and"). The most distinctiv
          +e words are: Tier 1 `arguments`, `take`, `type`, `inspect`; Ti
          +er 2 `update`, `callers`, `rename`, `calls`, `sites`; Tier 3 `
          +loop`, `fix`, `logic`, `implement`. These are close to the key
          +word lists, plus topic words like `loop` or `config` that only
          + happen to occur in one tier.                                 
      180 +- **Result:** Naive Bayes does **not** beat the keyword rule. 
          +On this split it is 23.3 points lower, and averaged over 50 sp
          +lits it is 59.4% against 69.2% (Block 8). Of its 16 holdout er
          +rors, the keyword rule got 9 right. The keyword rule stays in 
          +the server; a learned intent model would need many more, and r
          +eal, intents.                                                 
      181 +- **Evidence:** [`tests/test_intent_model.py`](tests/test_inte
          +nt_model.py): smoothing checked by hand on a 6-intent set, fit
          +ting only on train intents, all models scored on the same hold
          +out ids, metrics consistent with the confusion matrix, reprodu
          +cible report.                                                 
      182 +                                                              
      183 +### Block 8: Uncertainty on the Holdout (`src/acc_mcp/uncertai
          +nty.py`)                                                      
      184 +- **Why:** Blocks 4 and 7 quote one split with 30 holdout task
          +s, so a single accuracy cannot stand on its own.              
      185 +- **Methods (no new dependencies):** Wilson 95% intervals; per
          +centile bootstrap 95% intervals (2,000 resamples of the 30 hol
          +dout tasks, seed 8); paired comparisons on the same tasks, usi
          +ng the exact McNemar test (binomial on the discordant tasks) a
          +nd a paired bootstrap interval for the accuracy difference; an
          +d split sensitivity, where the stratified split and both fits 
          +are repeated for 50 seeds. Run `python3 src/acc_mcp/uncertaint
          +y.py` (about a minute, for the 50 weight fits).               
      186 +- **Holdout intervals (seed-21 split, Wilson 95%):**          
      187 +

      189 +  | :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: |                            
      190 +  | Logistic, fitted weights (Block 4) | 70.0% (52.1–83.3%) | 
          +14.3% (5.0–34.6%) | 33.3% (16.3–56.3%) |                      
      191 +  | Logistic, hand-picked weights | 66.7% (48.8–80.8%) | 28.6%
          + (13.8–50.0%) | 22.2% (9.0–45.2%) |

      149  - **What the fit shows:**
      150    - The tier is still decided almost entirely by $x_1$. With t
           he fitted weights, on the dataset $x_1 = 0$ gives $P \approx 0
           .1$–$0.22$ (Tier 1), $x_1 = 2$ gives $P \ge 0.9$ (Tier 3), and
            $x_1 = 1$ gives $P \approx 0.55$–$0.72$, which sits right on 
           $\theta_{\text{high}}$.
      151    - $x_2$ (complexity) and $x_4$ (annotations) get weights nea
           r zero. Setting either to 0 changes at most 3 train and 2 hold
           out tiers, because many $x_1 = 1$ tasks sit so close to $\thet
           a_{\text{high}}$ that a tiny shift moves them.

  ! .venv/bin/python -m pytest -q

  What the numbers say (all of this is now in the README)
  - Block 7, Naive Bayes vs the keyword rule: Naive Bayes is worse.
    - On the seed-21 holdout: 46.7% vs 70.0%. Averaged over 50 splits: 59.4% vs
      69.2%.
    - It overfits badly: 98.6% on its 70 training intents.
    - The words that point most to each tier are close to the keyword lists
      (callers, rename, fix, inspect), plus topic words that only happen to sit
      in one tier.
    - The keyword rule stays in the server.
  - Block 8, uncertainty:
    - Every holdout accuracy has a 95% interval about 30 points wide (the Block
      4 model's 70.0% is 52–83%).
    - Fitting the Block 4 weights didn't measurably help. It's +3.3 points over
      the hand-picked weights, with an interval of −20 to +27 and McNemar p =
      1.0. It traded misses (6 → 3) for false alarms (4 → 6).
    - Seed 21 was a lucky split for the logistic model: 70.0% there vs a 63.7%
      average over 50 splits. Over those splits, the keyword rule used directly
      as a tier (69.2%) beats the logistic model built on top of it.
    - Naive Bayes vs keyword rule: the paired bootstrap interval excludes 0, but
      McNemar gives p = 0.065. I described that as weak evidence, not a
      conclusion.

  - README Block 4 now has a short note sending readers to Block 8 before they
    trust the 70.0%.

⏺ Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
      21  
      22  **Adaptive Context Compressor (ACC-MCP)** is a Model Context Pr
          otocol (MCP) server that gives the agent **symbol-level navigat
          ion** instead. `find_symbol` says where a class, function or me
          thod is defined, and `get_adaptive_context` returns exactly tha
          t symbol at the depth the task needs: signatures (Tier 1), sign
          atures plus callers and callees (Tier 2), or the verbatim body 
          with its real line numbers (Tier 3). A **Decision Model** picks
           the tier from the task intent. Rules written in code enforce a
           token ceiling, a per-session token budget, and which files may
           be read at all.
      23  
      24 -Whether this makes a small model **cheaper at finding and readi
         -ng code without making it worse at fixing bugs** is what Block 
         -9 measures, against the agent's own `grep` + windowed `read` st
         -rategy (hypotheses H1-H3 in [`eval/PROTOCOL.md`](eval/PROTOCOL.
         -md)). Whole-file reading is not assumed; the benchmark counts i
         -t (large reads).                                               
      24 +Whether this makes a small model **cheaper at finding and readi
         +ng code without making it worse at fixing bugs** is the questio
         +n of the exploratory Pi benchmark in [`eval/`](eval/PROTOCOL.md
         +). That benchmark compares ACC-MCP with the agent's own `grep` 
         ++ windowed `read` strategy and counts whole-file reads instead 
         +of assuming them. It has only trace runs so far, so the project
         + claims no result from it (see "Exploratory: Pi Benchmark" belo
         +w).                                                            
      25  
      26  ---

           s before the first edit). It later called `get_adaptive_contex
           t` once more with `min_tier=3`. The run **resolved** the bug, 
           but only after a wrong first fix: 98 model calls and 22.6 minu
           tes in total, with 20 edits and 74 bash commands. This is one 
           run, a trace that the server works inside a real agent, not a 
           measurement.

      176  ### Block 5: Memory or Context Budget Management (`src/acc_mcp
           /budget.py`, `src/acc_mcp/index.py`)
      177  - **Session Budget:** All slices in a session share a token bu
           dget (`ACC_MCP_SESSION_BUDGET`, default 6,000 tokens, about 20
           % of the 30,208-token window; the benchmark sets exactly 20%, 
           6,042). Each slice is cut to $\min(C_{\max}, \text{tokens left
           })$. If the chosen tier does not fit, the server serves one ti
           er lower and says so in a `# [ACC-MCP] Served Tier n instead o
           f Tier m` note; a tier forced with `min_tier` that does not fi
           t is refused with a `# [ACC-MCP] Not served` message. `reset_s
           ession` starts a new budget.

⏺ Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
      177  - **Session Budget:** All slices in a session share a token bu
           dget (`ACC_MCP_SESSION_BUDGET`, default 6,000 tokens, about 20
           % of the 30,208-token window; the benchmark sets exactly 20%, 
           6,042). Each slice is cut to $\min(C_{\max}, \text{tokens left
           })$. If the chosen tier does not fit, the server serves one ti
           er lower and says so in a `# [ACC-MCP] Served Tier n instead o
           f Tier m` note; a tier forced with `min_tier` that does not fi
           t is refused with a `# [ACC-MCP] Not served` message. `reset_s
           ession` starts a new budget.
      178  - **Ledger:** Every request is recorded with its served tier, 
           model tier, `min_tier`, served tokens, and the line ranges it 
           showed (file, symbol, first and last line; for a slice cut by 
           the ceiling, only the lines really shown). The summary reports
            what the server really knows: served tokens per tier and per 
           tool (`get_adaptive_context`, `find_symbol`). The comparison w
           ith the whole file is kept only as a labelled **upper bound** 
           (`whole_file_tokens`, `upper_bound_savings_pct`):
      179    $$\text{Upper-bound saving (\%)} = \frac{\text{Tokens}_{\tex
           t{Whole File}} - \text{Tokens}_{\text{Served}}}{\text{Tokens}_
           {\text{Whole File}}} \times 100\%$$
      180 -  It is an upper bound because it assumes the agent would othe
          -rwise read the whole file, which it did not do in the pilot se
          -ssion. The saving that Block 9 tests is against the agent's ow
          -n `grep` + windowed `read` strategy. Ledgers written before th
          -is change (`baseline_tokens`, `savings_percentage`) still load
          -. The ledger is saved once per request to the file named by `A
          -CC_MCP_LEDGER`, or to `context_budget.json` in the server's wo
          -rking directory. Totals cover all requests; the history keeps 
          -only the last 200 records.                                    
      180 +  It is an upper bound because it assumes the agent would othe
          +rwise read the whole file, which it did not do in the pilot se
          +ssion. The exploratory benchmark compares with the agent's own
          + `grep` + windowed `read` strategy instead. Ledgers written be
          +fore this change (`baseline_tokens`, `savings_percentage`) sti
          +ll load. The ledger is saved once per request to the file name
          +d by `ACC_MCP_LEDGER`, or to `context_budget.json` in the serv
          +er's working directory. Totals cover all requests; the history
          + keeps only the last 200 records.                             
      181  - **Workspace Symbol Index (`index.py`):** Walks every servabl
           e `.py` file under the workspace root (skipping `.venv`, `venv
           `, `.git`, `__pycache__`, `node_modules`) and stores each file
           's symbol map (names, line ranges, complexities, callers, call
           ees, raised exceptions) in the ledger. Each file's SHA-256 is 
           compared with the cached `file_hash`: unchanged files are reus
           ed, changed files are re-parsed, deleted files are dropped.
      182  - **Cold Restart Test:** After a restart, the index is rebuilt
            from the ledger and unchanged files are not parsed again (the
            tests count parser calls). The file served by `get_adaptive_c
           ontext` is still parsed on each request, because slices are cu
           t from its AST.
      183  - **Evidence:** [`tests/test_budget.py`](tests/test_budget.py)
           , [`tests/test_index.py`](tests/test_index.py), and the budget
            tests in [`tests/test_server.py`](tests/test_server.py).

      183 +- **What a restart remembers, and what it costs (from the rest
          +art tests):** in the 3-file test workspace, a restarted server
          + re-indexes with **0 parses (3 files reused)**, and after one 
          +file is edited, with **1 parse (2 reused)**. All-time totals (
          +requests, served tokens per tier and per tool, escalations, ti
          +er counts) and the last 200 history records are restored exact
          +ly. The session budget starts full again (0 of 6,000 tokens us
          +ed), because a new server process means a new agent context. R
          +emembering costs the agent **0 tokens**: the ledger and the sy
          +mbol cache live on disk on the server side and never enter the
          + conversation. Only slices the agent asks for are counted.

      245 -### Block 9: A Measured Comparison (Manual Evaluation with Pi)
      246 -- **Question:** does ACC-MCP make it cheaper for a small local
          - model to find and read the code it needs, without making it w
          -orse at fixing the bug, when the model works inside a real cod
          -ing agent (Pi)? The full method is in [`eval/PROTOCOL.md`](eva
          -l/PROTOCOL.md).                                               
      247 -- **Hypotheses:**                                             
      248 -  - **H1 (navigation cost):** fewer navigation calls before th
          -e first edit of the target function, and fewer code-reading to
          -kens, than the agent's own `grep` + windowed `read` strategy. 
      249 -  - **H2 (success):** at least as many tasks resolved.        
      250 -  - **H3 (peak context):** a smaller largest prompt per model 
          -call. This may not change: in the pilot, most tool calls were 
          -the agent's own Python experiments, which ACC-MCP does not tou
          -ch.                                                           
      251 -- **Why our own benchmark:** SWE-bench's grader needs a Docker
          - image of several GB per task; Exercism-style exercises are si
          -ngle small files where ACC-MCP has nothing to save; HumanEval/
          -MBPP have no repository at all. The benchmark instead injects 
          -bugs into real libraries (the SWE-smith approach) and grades t
          -hem with the libraries' own tests, without Docker.            
      252 -- **Repositories:**                                           
      253 -                                                              
      254 -  | Repo | Pinned release | Files with injected bugs | Why |  
      255 -  | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |                               
      256 -  | [more-itertools](https://github.com/more-itertools/more-it
          -ertools) | `v11.2.1` | `more_itertools/more.py` (5,644 lines, 
          -~175 KB), `recipes.py` | One file of ~45K tokens, larger than 
          -the model's 30,208-token window, so the agent must find the ri
          -ght function. |                                               
      257 -  | [click](https://github.com/pallets/click) | `8.5.0` | `src
          -/click/core.py` (3,799 lines), `types.py`, `parser.py`, `forma
          -tting.py` | Classes and methods spread over modules, so caller
          -s and callees matter. |                                       
      258 -                                                              
      259 -- **Tasks (5 per repo):** each changes one line or condition i
          -n one function that has its own tests: `split_at`, `numeric_ra
          -nge.index`, `sliding_window`, `is_sorted`, `constrained_batche
          -s` (more-itertools); argument unpacking, `IntRange` minimum, e
          -ager-parameter order, `nargs=-1` envvar splitting, `--help` co
          -lumn layout (click). 6 tasks are **direct** (the report points
          - at the buggy function) and 4 are **call-graph** tasks (the re
          -port names a function whose helper has the bug, so the agent h
          -as to follow its callees). The agent gets only a bug report, n
          -ever the line, in a fresh repo whose only commit already conta
          -ins the bug. Each change, its target tests and its report are 
          -listed in the protocol.                                       
      260 -- **How a run is done:** by hand, in normal interactive Pi. Fo
          -r each task and mode, `prepare` builds the broken repo and a `
          -start.sh` that opens Pi with an isolated configuration; the op
          -erator pastes the bug report, watches the model work under fix
          -ed operator rules (no hints; only "Proceed as you think best."
          - / "Continue."), and quits when it is done or stuck. `collect`
          - then reads Pi's own session file for the measurements and run
          -s the repo's tests to check the fix.                          
      261 -- **Conditions:** every task is done twice with the same model
          -, prompt, tools, compaction settings, operator rules and time 
          -limit. Only the acc run adds the ACC-MCP server (tools declare
          -d directly) and one sentence telling the model to prefer it fo
          -r reading code.                                               
      262 -- **Grading:** after restoring the original tests, a task is r
          -esolved when the target tests pass and the full suite has no f
          -ailures beyond those of the clean repo.                       
      263 -- **Measured per run:** resolved or not; navigation calls befo
          -re the first edit of the target function, code-reading tokens,
          - wasted reads and large reads (the agent's own `grep` + window
          -ed `read` strategy is the baseline, not reading whole files); 
          -peak context of one model call, total prompt tokens, model cal
          -ls, tools used, compactions, wall time; and (with ACC-MCP) tie
          -rs served, explicit and silent escalations, and budget step-do
          -wns.                                                          
      264 -- **Helper script:** [`eval/pi_bench.py`](eval/pi_bench.py) (`
          -setup`, `validate`, `doctor`, `freeze`, `prepare`, `collect`, 
          -`list`, `report`). It never drives Pi itself. `freeze` records
          - the commit, model file, quantization, `llama-server` build, P
          -i version, window and budget before the counted runs, and `rep
          -ort` prints them with the results.                            
      265 -- **Status: not run yet.** No counted run exists; the only ses
          -sion so far is the pilot, which is excluded. Results will go t
          -o `eval/results.md`, and until then this README makes no claim
          - about navigation cost, token savings or task success.        
      245 +### Exploratory: Pi Benchmark (not a claimed block)           
      246 +- **What `eval/` measures:** a bug-injection benchmark on pinn
          +ed releases of more-itertools and click, with 10 tasks: 6 dire
          +ct and 4 call-graph tasks. Each task is done by hand in intera
          +ctive Pi, plain vs ACC-MCP, and graded by the libraries' own t
          +ests. Measured from Pi's session file: navigation calls before
          + the first edit of the target function, code-reading tokens, w
          +asted and large reads, and peak context; with ACC-MCP also tie
          +rs and explicit and silent escalations. The full method and hy
          +potheses are in [`eval/PROTOCOL.md`](eval/PROTOCOL.md), and th
          +e helper is [`eval/pi_bench.py`](eval/pi_bench.py) (`setup`, `
          +validate`, `doctor`, `freeze`, `prepare`, `collect`, `list`, `
          +report`).                                                     
      247 +- **What exists:** the pilot (plain, `mi_difference`; it showe
          +d the agent greps and reads line windows instead of whole file
          +s) and trace runs of `mi_split_at` in both modes. Both trace r
          +uns resolved the bug. The acc run is the real-agent trace quot
          +ed under Block 2.                                             
      248 +- **Why it is not claimed:** comparing two modes with an unsee
          +ded model needs at least 10 tasks × 3 runs per mode (about 60 
          +hand runs of up to 30 minutes each). One run per task cannot s
          +eparate ACC-MCP from run-to-run noise: the single `mi_split_at
          +` pair differs by a factor of 9 in model calls (11 plain, 98 a
          +cc), mostly because of one wrong first fix. So no navigation, 
          +token or success result is claimed from it.                   
      249  - An earlier simulated benchmark was removed: it marked every 
           gated run as a success without checking the model's answer, an
           d one generated file produced most of its reported saving.

      10  Small local models such as **Qwen3.5-4B** run with a small cont
          ext window on a laptop (this project uses 30,208 tokens, the pe
          r-slot window `llama-server` reports on the reference machine).
           Inside a coding agent, the model finds code by **navigating**:
           it searches with `grep`/`find` and reads files in line windows
          , some of which miss the code it needs. Each search and each wi
          ndow is a tool call whose output stays in the conversation. In 
          the pilot session (excluded from the results) the agent never r
          ead the ~45K-token file whole. It found the function with `grep
          ` and read it in 6 windows of 10-100 lines, 2 of which missed t
          he function.
      11  
      12 -**Adaptive Context Compressor (ACC-MCP)** is a standalone Model
         - Context Protocol (MCP) server that gives the agent **symbol-le
         -vel navigation**. `find_symbol` locates a definition, and `get_
         -adaptive_context` serves exactly that symbol at the depth the t
         -ask needs. An embedded **Decision Model** picks that depth (sig
         -natures, call graph, or verbatim body), and a deterministic cod
         -e layer enforces token ceilings, a session budget and file secu
         -rity boundaries. Block 9 measures whether this makes finding an
         -d reading code cheaper than the agent's own `grep` + windowed `
         -read` strategy, without lowering the success rate.             
      12 +**Adaptive Context Compressor (ACC-MCP)** is a standalone Model
         + Context Protocol (MCP) server that gives the agent **symbol-le
         +vel navigation**. `find_symbol` locates a definition, and `get_
         +adaptive_context` serves exactly that symbol at the depth the t
         +ask needs. An embedded **Decision Model** picks that depth (sig
         +natures, call graph, or verbatim body), and a deterministic cod
         +e layer enforces token ceilings, a session budget and file secu
         +rity boundaries. An exploratory Pi benchmark (`eval/`) is built
         + to measure whether this makes finding and reading code cheaper
         + than the agent's own `grep` + windowed `read` strategy, withou
         +t lowering the success rate. It has only trace runs so far, so 
         +no result is claimed from it (section 5).

      131  ### Block 5: Memory or Context Budget Management
      132  * **Implementation:** A per-session token budget plus a ledger
            that persists across server restarts.

⏺ Update(Description.md)
  ⎿  Added 19 lines, removed 12 lines
      134    * Caps the tokens served per session (20% of the window in t
           he benchmark) and steps down a tier when the chosen one does n
           ot fit.
      135    * Persists the request history (with the line ranges each sl
           ice showed), totals per tier and per tool, and the workspace s
           ymbol index to a local `context_budget.json` ledger. The whole
           -file comparison is kept only as an upper bound (section 4.4).
      136    * Restart test: after a restart the symbol index is rebuilt 
           from the ledger and unchanged files are not parsed again (the 
           file being served is still parsed for each request).
      137 +  * What a restart remembers and costs, from the tests: in the
          + 3-file test workspace a restart re-indexes with 0 parses (3 f
          +iles reused), or 1 parse after one file is edited. All-time to
          +tals and the last 200 history records come back exactly, and t
          +he session budget starts full. The agent pays 0 tokens for thi
          +s memory, because the ledger and symbol cache stay on the serv
          +er's disk.

          - unpacking, eager order, `--help` columns), where the report n

          -can matter.
